[0:19]: let's start with the goals of a case 
[0:21]: study. The very first one is to show 
[0:23]: people what problems you've identified

[0:32]: you.

[0:38]: specifically around achieving those two 
[0:40]: goals. Let's hop into step one and get 
[0:42]: this started. So step number one is going 
[0:44]: to be to have a strong definition of the 
[0:47]: problem and a brief snapshot of what 
[0:49]: your solution looks like. In a case study,

[0:53]: nailed down before you can go anywhere 
[0:55]: else. Without a strong problem statement, 
[0:57]: by identifying the wrong problem or even

[6:52]: approach it. Now, font sizing is where 
[6:55]: things can get more interesting. That's 
[6:57]: because while font colors, weights, and 
[6:59]: pairings can stay constant on any screen, 
[7:02]: fonts change size based on the screen

[7:08]: them. Let's start out simple with just 
[7:10]: one desktop screen. Even with just one

[7:27]: many designers do things like that. 
[7:29]: It's not wrong, but it can be improved. To 
[7:32]: do that, we need to learn more about this 
[7:34]: dude, Leonardo Bonacci, better known as 
[7:36]: Fibonacci, and his sequence. You've 
[7:39]: probably heard about this. The sequence 
[7:41]: starts with two numbers—let's say 1 and 
[7:43]: 2—and adds them together to get 3. 
[7:45]: Then it takes 2 and 3 and adds 
[7:47]: them together to get 5. Then it takes 
[7:49]: 3 and 5 and adds them together to 
[7:51]: get 8, and so on. We can take these 
[7:53]: numbers and assign them to side lengths 
[7:55]: of rectangles like so. If we expand this 
[7:58]: out a couple times and draw an arc 
[8:00]: around the outside of each rectangle, we 
[8:03]: get this spiral which is seen in a 
[8:04]: shocking amount of places in nature, such 
[8:06]: as sunflowers, pine cones, and seashells. 
[8:10]: As these numbers increase in size, we can 
[8:12]: take the larger number and divide it by 
[8:14]: the smaller of the two numbers to get an 
[8:16]: approximation of the Golden Ratio, which 
[8:19]: is 1.62. Phew! Okay, that was some math, but 
[8:23]: how does this help us with font sizing? 
[8:25]: Well, in the most basic of scenarios 
[8:27]: where we have a header and some

[8:31]: text by 1.62 to get the size of the

[8:37]: across an entire style guide with many 
[8:40]: different font sizes. If we start with 
[8:42]: a 16-pixel base font size and multiply 
[8:45]: each size by 1.62, our font sizes start 
[8:48]: to get very large a little too quickly.

[8:57]: modify this a little bit; after all, to go

[9:18]: the Golden Ratio. This is useful if

[10:18]: text on our smallest mobile screen. We

[10:21]: largest and smallest font size and

[10:26]: screen size. This is the difference 
[10:27]: between our current screen size and our 
[10:29]: smallest screen size over the difference 
[10:32]: between our largest and smallest screen 
[10:34]: size. I generally use 1920 as the largest 
[10:37]: width and 320 as the smallest width, but 
[10:40]: you can play around with those numbers. 
[10:42]: The graph for this equation looks 
[10:44]: something like this. Those of you with a

[10:48]: past our smallest and largest size,

[10:55]: size. If that works in the design,

[0:00]: Anybody can make good designs, but it's 
[0:02]: these tiny details that make them 
[0:04]: exceptional. Like why your text looks 
[0:06]: weird when you scale it up, or why your 
[0:08]: rounded corners feel off, and the 
[0:10]: secret to picking color palettes that 
[0:12]: actually look good without guessing. 
[0:15]: And if you stick around, you'll see these UI 
[0:16]: tips used everywhere by the best 
[0:18]: designers and companies. Let's get into it. 
[0:21]: First up is kerning on large 
[0:23]: text. Kerning is the space between 
[0:26]: letters, and you know, on most smaller 
[0:28]: text sizes, it looks fine. Figma 
[0:30]: automatically sorts that out for you, but 
[0:32]: on larger text sizes, generally over 228
[0:35]: to 80 pixels, it starts to matter more. 
[0:37]: Here's a design I made a little while 
[0:38]: ago in this video, and right now we've 
[0:41]: got zero kerning on it. I just eyeball this, 
[0:43]: but generally -2% to -4% is a 
[0:47]: reasonable amount. Let's check this out 
[0:49]: side-by-side so you can see it better. 
[0:51]: It's sort of subtle, but I'd find nice 
[0:54]: fonts that I'd use as paragraphs and 
[0:56]: they look great, but I'd scale it up and 
[0:58]: it'd look disjointed, and I'd just blame it on 
[1:00]: the font, as one does. Especially with the 
[1:03]: rise of larger text on websites, this is 
[1:05]: a pro tip to know. Next up, we're fixing 
[1:08]: those rounded corners. Modern design has 
[1:11]: almost a fetish with rounded 
[1:13]: corners, and I gotta say, I'm one of 
[1:16]: those designers too. So if you insist on 
[1:18]: rounding everything, then let's at least 
[1:20]: do it right. See, when we have one corner, 
[1:23]: everything is fine and dandy, but if we 
[1:25]: put a corner inside a corner, then we can 
[1:27]: see the issue. Everything is equal on the 
[1:30]: straight edges, but as soon as we come to 
[1:31]: the corner, the distance increases, and 
[1:34]: for us designers who are OCD about every 
[1:36]: pixel, this ain't going to slide. The fix 
[1:39]: is easy: just round the inner corner less. 
[1:41]: Now, the current rule is that you take 
[1:43]: the outer corner radius and subtract the 
[1:45]: distance between the two corners. So if 
[1:48]: the outside is a 30-pixel radius and 
[1:50]: we've got a 10-pixel gap, then we'll 
[1:52]: round the inner corner by 20 pixels. This, 
[1:54]: of course, breaks down as soon as the 
[1:56]: inner corner is more than 30 pixels away, 
[1:58]: so I just prefer to squint and guesstimate. 
[2:01]: Importantly, if you've got a pill shape like 
[2:03]: this, then you don't need to do any of 
[2:04]: this, since the distance is the same all 
[2:06]: the way around. And if you really want to 
[2:08]: lean into the trend, we can actually make 
[2:10]: your rounded corners rounder. And no, this 
[2:13]: isn't like when a client asks you to 
[2:15]: make the blue bluer. Go into Figma, hit 
[2:17]: the four corners, and dial up the iOS 
[2:20]: corner smoothing to the max to very 
[2:22]: subtly taper the corners before hitting 
[2:24]: the corner. It's difficult to see, but if 
[2:26]: I stack one with iOS smoothing and one 
[2:28]: without, you can see right here where the 
[2:30]: difference is. Now you might be thinking, 
[2:32]: "How can I stay on top of all these tiny

[3:28]: base color. If you can't figure out a good base

[3:55]: decrease the brightness by another 10. 
[3:58]: But we can make this even better by 
[3:59]: adjusting the hue for each of our colors. 
[4:02]: So again, we'll start with our base color. 
[4:04]: For our folder color, we're going to 
[4:05]: choose a slightly darker hue. Blues and 
[4:08]: purples tend to be the darkest, while 
[4:09]: yellows and reds are the lightest. So we 
[4:12]: slide towards blue about 20 points. Then, 
[4:15]: same exact idea from above: we're going 
[4:17]: to increase our saturation by 20 and 
[4:19]: decrease our brightness by 10, and do the 
[4:21]: same thing again for our strip here.

[5:25]: Tip five: lose the 
[5:28]: lines. Unless you're creating UI that has 
[5:31]: lines as its style, like this website 
[5:33]: here, lines are often redundant and add 
[5:35]: clutter to a design. If it's just a few 
[5:38]: lines like from the previous example, 
[5:40]: that's less problematic, but when you 
[5:41]: have a list of items like this, the best 
[5:43]: option is just to space them far enough 
[5:45]: apart that it is legible and they're clearly 
[5:48]: separate. See how much more difficult it 
[5:50]: is to read them as we pack them closer 
[5:52]: and closer together. If you do have to 
[5:54]: space them tightly for whatever reason, 
[5:56]: using a subtle background on alternating 
[5:58]: rows is a better choice than lines 
[6:00]: everywhere. The fewer elements you can 
[6:02]: use to get your point across, the better. 
[6:05]: Six: keep your spacing consistent. Easily 
[6:08]: one of the most important parts of 
[6:09]: consistency is spacing, which is 
[6:11]: typically done with a 4-pixel or 8-pixel 
[6:14]: base grid. Especially for smaller 
[6:16]: elements like the short-term rental card 
[6:18]: we created above, it definitely makes 
[6:19]: things look noticeably more organized. If 
[6:22]: you're working with an 8-pixel grid, then go 
[6:24]: to your Figma settings, preferences, and 
[6:26]: then nudge amount. Change that from the 
[6:28]: default of 10 to eight so you always 
[6:30]: stay aligned with your grid even when 
[6:32]: making small adjustments. When working 
[6:33]: with larger sizes, I typically round to 
[6:35]: the nearest 5 or 10, but if you need to 
[6:38]: stick to 8 pixels, make your sizes 
[6:40]: exponentially larger, since the 
[6:42]: difference between 120 and 128 just 
[6:45]: isn't going to make a difference. Seven: 
[6:47]: use a darker version of your accent 
[6:49]: color instead of pure white or pure 
[6:51]: black as a background to incorporate 
[6:53]: more color into your designs. For example, 
[6:56]: on GitHub and a lot of other dark mode

[0:00]: I've spent years making bad designs, so 
[0:02]: I can tell you exactly what makes 
[0:03]: something look like it was made by a 
[0:05]: beginner. By the end of this video, 
[0:07]: you'll be able to spot those mistakes 
[0:08]: instantly and fix them just as fast. 
[0:11]: We'll look at real examples, before-and-afters, 
[0:13]: and easy ways to improve a UI 
[0:15]: without starting from scratch. We're 
[0:17]: going to redesign this simple recipe app 
[0:19]: one mistake at a time until it looks 
[0:21]: polished and production-ready. Mistake 
[0:23]: number one: user flow. Let's start with 
[0:26]: something that quietly ruins your UX: 
[0:28]: user flow. Here's our allergy screen 
[0:30]: with six preset options, but no search 
[0:33]: bar if someone's allergic to something 
[0:34]: else. We also don't have a skip button. 
[0:37]: What if the user doesn't have 
[0:39]: any allergies? These are things you will 
[0:41]: miss if you don't plan out your flow, 
[0:42]: but users will feel it instantly. 
[0:45]: So, even something as simple as 
[0:46]: sketching boxes on paper helps you catch 
[0:48]: major gaps early on. And it's not 
[0:51]: all about big stuff. Little touches like 
[0:53]: a filter icon on the search bar or a 
[0:55]: save button up top go a long way. The 
[0:58]: most commonly missed elements are 
[0:59]: navigation links, hidden states, and 
[1:01]: dynamic interactions like button hovers 
[1:04]: or micro-interactions. Mistake number 
[1:06]: two: overusing effects. Early on, I was 
[1:09]: addicted to visual effects. Shadows, 
[1:12]: glows, gradients—you name it. But the 
[1:14]: truth is, overusing them can make your 
[1:16]: designs feel cluttered and amateur. 
[1:18]: Let's start with the gradient here. It's 
[1:20]: blue and green, and it's just not 
[1:23]: working. If you really need a gradient, 
[1:25]: stick to variations of the same color, 
[1:27]: like a darker or lighter green. But 
[1:29]: honestly, this would look way cleaner 
[1:31]: with no gradient at all. And that's 
[1:33]: usually the case. Now, let's talk drop 
[1:35]: shadows for a second. These Figma 
[1:37]: defaults are way too harsh. Instead of 
[1:40]: just lowering opacity, I change the 
[1:42]: entire shadow color, usually to a light 
[1:44]: gray, and increase the blur 
[1:46]: significantly. Or once again, remove the 
[1:49]: shadow completely. Because most of the 
[1:51]: time, less visual noise equals a better 
[1:54]: design. Mistake number three: spacing. 
[1:57]: Most beginner UIs are packed way too 
[1:59]: tight, like my old app design. I'll 
[2:01]: use two tools here—grids and auto 
[2:03]: layout—to make it so much better. First, 
[2:05]: we'll set up a three-column grid for 
[2:07]: this screen and a two-column grid here 
[2:09]: with these measurements. Now, just align 
[2:11]: elements to the grid as closely as 
[2:13]: possible. If something needs to break 
[2:15]: the grid slightly, like these elements, 
[2:17]: just leave it as is if it still feels 
[2:19]: balanced. Next, for stacked content like 
[2:21]: this, increase the vertical spacing to 
[2:23]: let things breathe and group naturally. 
[2:25]: Especially on mobile screens, you'll 
[2:27]: need more space than you think. Now, 
[2:29]: check out these cards and chips. The 
[2:31]: layouts are pretty wonky. It is the perfect time 
[2:33]: to use auto layout. Turn off vertical 
[2:36]: trim for pixel-perfect control and 
[2:38]: adjust spacing like this. Repeat this 
[2:40]: across your UI to keep spacing 
[2:42]: consistent and clean, which is a perfect 
[2:45]: segue into the next mistake most 
[2:47]: designers make. Mistake number four: 
[2:49]: inconsistent components. Like spacing, 
[2:52]: this one kills me since it's just so 
[2:54]: easy to fix. You'll notice throughout 
[2:55]: our design, we have all sorts of 
[2:57]: different corner radiuses and components, 
[3:00]: like this back and skip button, that are 
[3:02]: exactly the same but designed 
[3:04]: differently, which just makes the design 
[3:06]: feel amateur. Instead, let's make a few 
[3:08]: changes. First, we'll make the two 
[3:10]: search bars identical other than the 
[3:12]: prompting text. We'll set the corner 
[3:14]: radius of all smaller components to be 
[3:16]: 10 pixels. This is an important one that 
[3:18]: most designers miss. And of course, 
[3:21]: we'll update the skip button and back 
[3:22]: button to match in size, corner radius, 
[3:24]: and style. Being consistent means using 
[3:27]: styles for colors, variables for 
[3:29]: measurements, and components for UI 
[3:31]: elements to really bring your design 
[3:32]: together. Mistake number five: icons. I 
[3:36]: think people underestimate how much good 
[3:38]: icons do for a design and how 
[3:40]: detrimental bad icons are. First of all, 
[3:43]: we have no icons on these cards here, 
[3:46]: which forces users to read more and 
[3:48]: slows down the browsing process. Let's 
[3:50]: add in some good icons and replace the 
[3:52]: save text. Then up above, we have icons, 
[3:55]: but they're mismatched in fill, line 
[3:57]: width, and style. We can fix this by 
[3:59]: using the same library of icons, like 
[4:02]: Flaticon. On their website, select 
[4:04]: interface icons and then sort by stroke, 
[4:06]: width, and corner to get some consistent 
[4:08]: results. Make sure when you download to 
[4:10]: grab SVG for the best results. If you 
[4:13]: want to choose a Figma plugin instead, I 
[4:15]: like Feather Icons and Phosphor Icons. 
[4:18]: Finally, we have a fair amount of icons 
[4:20]: down here without labels. If the icons 
[4:22]: are well-known enough, like the house, 
[4:24]: bookmark, and user, then you don't need 
[4:26]: to have anything; but for these two, a 
[4:28]: tooltip pop-up could make the 
[4:30]: onboarding process much smoother. Just 
[4:32]: try not to have bizarre icons 
[4:34]: everywhere. Finally, this is a pro 
[4:36]: tip, but interestingly, it is okay to 
[4:39]: have different styles of icons in the 
[4:41]: same design, but only if they're 
[4:42]: visually separate. For example, these 
[4:44]: navbar icons are slightly different than 
[4:47]: the food icons, and those are different 
[4:48]: than our recipe icons, but it all works 
[4:51]: together since they're in separate areas 
[4:52]: of our design and used for different 
[4:54]: things. Mistake number six: redundant 
[4:57]: elements. Having redundant elements is 
[4:59]: actually a really common mistake, 
[5:01]: especially as the trend of having stuff 
[5:03]: everywhere takes over. Looking at this 
[5:05]: screen, we can get rid of this arrow 
[5:07]: since we'll be able to swipe on mobile. 
[5:09]: And same idea down below—we can also 
[5:11]: get rid of this arrow since it's really 
[5:13]: just visual clutter and dim down the 
[5:15]: color of the button. You could probably 
[5:16]: argue that these arrows aren't needed 
[5:18]: either, but we are going to keep those. 
[5:20]: Finally, the stroke on these elements is 
[5:22]: really not needed. If you are worried 
[5:24]: about contrast ratios, dimming it down 
[5:26]: significantly is a good option too, but 
[5:29]: we'll just rip them straight out. 
[5:30]: Mistake number seven: interactive 
[5:32]: feedback. We've covered overusing 
[5:34]: effects; so now let's cover the 
[5:36]: underuse of different interactive 
[5:38]: effects. For example, if we're 
[5:39]: navigating through our flow, but it 
[5:41]: takes a split second longer for the next

[0:00]: Most designers want to work for Big Tech 
[0:01]: or start an agency, but most designers 
[0:04]: won't. Most designers focus on making 
[0:06]: things look good, but genius designers 
[0:08]: don't. They focus on making things work 
[0:10]: brilliantly for the user. Think of web 
[0:12]: design not as art, but as a story, and 
[0:15]: not every story needs 3D graphics or 
[0:17]: buttery smooth scrolling. If your goal 
[0:19]: is to impress other designers, maybe. 
[0:21]: But if your goal is to guide someone to 
[0:23]: an action, all that flare might just be 
[0:25]: in the way. Start with user intent. 
[0:27]: Let's say you need to design a user flow 
[0:28]: that enables customers to rent vacation 
[0:31]: properties, not unlike Airbnb. A UI 
[0:33]: designer might start thinking about the 
[0:35]: icons or the layout of each card and 
[0:37]: entirely lose the plot of why we're 
[0:39]: designing. The intent of the user is to 
[0:41]: search for accommodations. So, a search 
[0:43]: bar is a natural starting point. We can 
[0:45]: then augment with useful features like 
[0:47]: destination, amount of travelers, and 
[0:49]: dates. You might think the next step is 
[0:50]: to put a header above this and slap an 
[0:52]: image on the background. But think about 
[0:54]: the user. Does this design do anything 
[0:56]: extra that this design didn't? Not at 
[0:59]: all. But there can be more than one 
[1:01]: intent for a website. Maybe the user knows 
[1:03]: they want to go on a vacation but 
[1:05]: doesn't know where. They're there to 
[1:07]: browse. We can serve that intent too by 
[1:09]: displaying our listings, but the user 
[1:12]: probably wants something more specific. 
[1:14]: So, we can add in filtering UI elements. 
[1:17]: Notice how the fonts, colors, and icons 
[1:19]: affect the aesthetics, but do very 
[1:21]: little to change the functionality. Only 
[1:23]: as the user intent expands do we expand 
[1:25]: the functionality of our designs. But we 
[1:27]: jump the gun a little by building full 
[1:29]: UIs before talking about leveraging 
[1:31]: existing layouts. Why not put the search 
[1:33]: bar down here or arrange the cards like 
[1:35]: this? Truth be told, nothing is stopping 
[1:37]: us from making a UI like this if it 
[1:39]: serves the user better. But after thirty 
[1:41]: plus years of websites, users have come 
[1:43]: to expect certain layouts. This doesn't 
[1:45]: preclude us from trying new layouts, but 
[1:47]: it means that typically information should 
[1:49]: flow top to bottom and left to right. 
[1:51]: Navigation is at the top of the page, 
[1:52]: and calls to action are typically 
[1:54]: eye-catching and easy to find. Designs 
[1:56]: that follow these guides are generally 
[1:58]: much easier to integrate new sections 
[2:00]: seamlessly and make responsive. 
[2:02]: Respecting these expectations while 
[2:04]: making the design uniquely yours, like 
[2:06]: adding a cool micro-interaction or a set 
[2:08]: of unique features, is what separates the 
[2:10]: good from the genius. And if you're 
[2:12]: trying to think like a genius designer, 
[2:14]: it helps to see how the best are already 
[2:16]: thinking. Mobbin is a curated library of 
[2:19]: real-world UI and UX, from full user flows 
[2:22]: down to individual components, all pulled 
[2:25]: from top apps and websites. Instead of 
[2:27]: downloading dozens of apps just to 
[2:29]: reverse engineer their design patterns, 
[2:31]: Mobbin lets you search and explore them 
[2:33]: all in one place. Personally, I'm always 
[2:35]: diving into the UI element section. 
[2:37]: There's a link in the description if you 
[2:38]: do want to check it out. Thank you to 
[2:40]: Mobbin for sponsoring this video. But 
[2:41]: without a content structure, design has 
[2:43]: very little value. There are two steps 
[2:45]: to designing around content. The first 
[2:48]: is to decide which content to display. 
[2:50]: Understanding how the user will interact 
[2:52]: with the design will determine what type 
[2:54]: of information is presented. For 
[2:56]: example, on our travel page, we can 
[2:57]: include a short description for every 
[2:59]: listing. But if the user wants a 
[3:01]: detailed look, they'll just click on the 
[3:02]: listing. They need the specifics to scan, 
[3:05]: like the location, rating, and price, 
[3:07]: before anything else. The second step is 
[3:09]: structuring the content that we do 
[3:10]: display. Currently, our design has 
[3:12]: perfect content, meaning that the titles 
[3:15]: and locations are all short. But what if 
[3:17]: we had a destination with a really long 
[3:19]: name or save icons on really bright 
[3:21]: listing images? We'd probably want to 
[3:23]: truncate long names and add a circle to 
[3:26]: the icon to guarantee contrast. 
[3:28]: Otherwise, you end up with unintended 
[3:30]: consequences, which are not fun to deal 
[3:32]: with. Animations should add clarity or 
[3:34]: functionality to a design. Just take a 
[3:36]: look at my old portfolio website. It's 
[3:39]: awful. All of these animations didn't do 
[3:41]: anything. Navigation is a great example 
[3:43]: of this. As we add more links, we simply 
[3:46]: run out of room. So, we consolidate them 
[3:48]: into a menu that can animate in, serving 
[3:50]: a purpose. Or if our web app starts to 
[3:52]: take on a browsing priority, instead of 
[3:55]: sticking this block to the top of the 
[3:56]: screen, we can reduce the size of the 
[3:58]: search bar and have it animate in when 
[4:01]: clicked. These are all great examples of 
[4:03]: progressive disclosure: showing the user 
[4:05]: what they need to see and revealing more 
[4:07]: as it's required. That's why a "load more" 
[4:09]: button is preferable over infinite 
[4:11]: scroll. It gives the user more control 
[4:13]: and, crucially, it lets them actually 
[4:16]: reach the footer, something infinite 
[4:18]: scroll often makes impossible. Context 
[4:20]: matters, but as a general rule, buttons 
[4:22]: should almost always have a small 
[4:24]: animation, while scroll-jacking effects 
[4:26]: should be used very sparingly, if ever. 
[4:28]: Finally, design systems add a layer of 
[4:31]: replicability and speed that's tough to 
[4:33]: match. But the system has to reflect the 
[4:35]: values of the team. A lean startup might 
[4:37]: need something lightweight and flexible, 
[4:39]: quick to update and easy to scrap, 
[4:42]: whereas something like Google's Material 
[4:43]: Design is massive and deeply defined 
[4:46]: because the use cases span dozens of 
[4:48]: products and billions of users. More 
[4:50]: importantly, the development of a design 
[4:52]: system is often more critical than the 
[4:54]: design itself. The process of defining 
[4:57]: rules, spacing, typography scales, and 
[4:59]: interaction patterns gives you an 
[5:01]: architecture for expansion. But a design 
[5:03]: system isn't about making everything 
[5:05]: look the same. It's about giving your 
[5:07]: team a shared language. And like any 
[5:09]: language, mastery means knowing when to 
[5:10]: bend the rules. You don't break the 
[5:12]: system by accident; you break it with 
[5:14]: intention. Overall, I hope this gave 
[5:16]: some perspective on how to think like a 
[5:18]: better designer. If you're interested in 
[5:19]: checking out Mobbin, it'll be the first 
[5:21]: link in the description below. And 
[5:23]: thanks for watching.

[0:00]: Let's start simple. You don't need a 
[0:01]: boot camp, fancy software, or a $3,145
[0:04]: laptop to break into UI/UX in 2025. 
[0:07]: Here's the roadmap on how to actually 
[0:08]: get on the inside. Most people start by 
[0:10]: learning UI design. They learn how to 
[0:12]: make a landing page, style a button, 
[0:14]: maybe even design a portfolio, and then 
[0:16]: they hit a wall. Because no matter how 
[0:18]: polished the visuals are, no one's 
[0:19]: hiring them just for that. Think of UI 
[0:22]: design like learning to decorate a 
[0:23]: single room. Product design is more like 
[0:25]: learning how to design the entire house: 
[0:28]: the flow, the function, the wiring, and 
[0:30]: how it all connects. Let's take Linear 
[0:32]: as a good example. It looks beautiful, 
[0:34]: but that's not why people love it. The 
[0:36]: real strength in Linear is its clarity. 
[0:38]: The states, the flows, and the system 
[0:40]: make it feel effortless. Hover over an 
[0:42]: issue, and you get context. Create a new 
[0:45]: project, and there's an empty state guiding 
[0:47]: you. Even the status animations are 
[0:49]: subtle but meaningful. And if you can 
[0:51]: put together a portfolio with products 
[0:53]: that solve a problem like this one, 
[0:55]: you'll be so much more likely to land a 
[0:57]: job. And that's the power of product 
[0:59]: design. It really just revolves 
[1:01]: around three key ideas. First of all, 
[1:03]: most designers just design the "happy 
[1:04]: path"—the perfect screen. But real apps 
[1:07]: need to handle all the states users 
[1:09]: actually experience: empty states, 
[1:11]: loading states, success messages—tiny 
[1:14]: moments that make or break the

[1:17]: dub.co. When you land on the dashboard,

[1:28]: you deploy a project, you're not just 
[1:30]: guessing if it worked. Immediately, you 
[1:32]: get a notification about the build 
[1:34]: status, so users are never left 
[1:35]: guessing. These aren't just UI details. 
[1:38]: They're design decisions that keep users 
[1:40]: informed and in control. If we were to 
[1:42]: apply that to our own hiring management 
[1:44]: tool, we can start with the sidebar.

[1:57]: no hires to manage and a call to action

[2:02]: for flow. Instead of thinking in 
[2:04]: sections like you would for a website,

[2:08]: would for a real application. That might

[1:58]: a dashboard to a landing page, you'll 
[1:59]: see the features of a dashboard are much 
[2:01]: smaller because we're trying to shove in 
[2:03]: many more things. These are the text styles 
[2:05]: for the landing page, and these are the 
[2:06]: text styles for the dashboard. We've got a 
[2:09]: lot of smaller font sizes without much 
[2:10]: spacing in between sizes. Grids and 
[2:13]: layouts are also much more strictly 
[2:14]: followed for dashboards than landing 
[2:16]: pages because we're using most or all of 
[2:18]: the space on screen. Third and final, 
[2:20]: what you put in this main section of the 
[2:22]: dashboard reflects what is most 
[2:24]: important to the user. For example, if 
[2:26]: you're building a project management 
[2:27]: dashboard, displaying the project status 
[2:29]: would be very useful. Or if you're 
[2:31]: building a financial dashboard, you

[2:42]: important metrics down below. The very 
[2:44]: top of the dashboard is generally 
[2:45]: reserved for important page actions or 
[2:47]: simple navigation. For our relatively 
[2:49]: simple dashboard, we'll have a dropdown 
[2:51]: and a button to make a link, which seems 
[2:54]: pretty important. For any data displayed

[2:59]: favicon, the shortened link, the actual

[5:38]: which frequently get missed. Finally, if 
[5:40]: the context is more permanent or is very 
[5:42]: large, like clicking on an existing

[5:46]: to a new page. If you're going to do

[0:17]: always more with color. Not everything 
[0:19]: should be a different color like it is

[0:23]: hole in my eye, and they almost all fail 
[0:25]: the WCAG contrast checks, meaning we 
[0:27]: can't reasonably use them. I'm sure 
[0:29]: you've heard of the 60-30-10 rule, meaning 
[0:31]: 60% is our dominant neutral color, 
[0:34]: 30% secondary color, and 10% accent

[0:39]: colorful example of this, with purple
